U.S. TOURISM WANTS CANADIANS BACK. THE TRADE FIGHT IS MAKING THAT HARDER.

American tourism groups are offering Canadians discounts and new outreach, but the rupture in Canadaâ€“U.S. relations remains a costly obstacle for destinations that rely on cross-border visitors.

ET CANADA AT WAR Latest About & methodology Corrections & contact AI-generated editorial illustration. Canadaâ€“U.S. relations Â· Tourism Â· Consumer response U.S. TOURISM WANTS CANADIANS BACK. THE TRADE FIGHT IS MAKING THAT HARDER. U.S. destinations are offering discounts and new outreach to Canadians. But after a year of lost trips, a trade fight and political insults are making a quick return unlikely. Published Sept. 6, 2026 Â· 10:39 a.m. ET OTTAWA â€” American tourism groups are spending money to bring Canadians back. They are offering hotel and attraction discounts, meeting travel advisers north of the border and taking their pitch directly to a market that once supplied the United States with more overnight international visitors than any other country. The problem is that the sales pitch is running into a political rupture that a lower room rate cannot easily solve. Canadian travel to the United States fell sharply in 2025 as Donald Trump returned to office, spoke repeatedly about absorbing Canada and launched tariffs. The breakdown in trade talks late last month has given many Canadians another reason to keep their spending elsewhere. The Associated Press reported this weekend that New York State has promoted a Canada-focused package of hotel, restaurant and attraction offers; some downtown Las Vegas hotels are treating the Canadian dollar at par; and the Las Vegas tourism authority travelled to Vancouver to meet the travel trade. Brand USA will bring a renamed trade-event series to Canada in October. Tourism money is part of the economic relationship Canadaâ€™s pullback is not symbolic to the places that depend on it. Statistics Canada found that Canadian residents made 25.4 per cent fewer return border crossings from the United States in 2025 than in 2024. The agency said Canadians made 3.2 million fewer leisure visits to the United States while taking more leisure trips overseas. The U.S. Travel Association had already warned that even a 10-per-cent reduction in Canadian travel could mean two million fewer visits, US$2.1 billion less spending and 14,000 fewer American jobs. Its estimate was a forecast, not a count of losses, but it shows why tourism offices are now working so openly to repair the market. There have been some signs of movement this summer. Statistics Canada reported that Canadian return trips from the United States rose in May and June, and the World Cup brought extra cross-border traffic. But the recovery has been uneven: the agency said air travel to the United States was still below its year-earlier level through June, while the recent increase was driven mainly by road trips. A discount cannot settle a political dispute The latest outreach comes as Ottawa prepares counter-tariffs on C$27.6 billion in American imports for Sept. 8, in response to the U.S. duties imposed in August. The measures cover goods from steel and dairy to appliances, farm equipment, pulp and paper and electronics. For Canadians who have changed their travel plans, the issue is broader than the price of a Florida hotel or a Las Vegas show. It is whether to direct vacation money to a country whose president has treated Canada as a target for tariffs and political taunts. The tourism industry cannot control that argument, but it has to live with the result. Analysis: The tourism campaign is a practical reminder that Canadaâ€™s response is not confined to government policy. A family choosing Mexico, Europe or a Canadian holiday over a U.S. trip makes no customs declaration and pays no tariff. But repeated millions of times, that decision becomes a real cost for American communities â€” and another link in the old relationship that may not automatically return. That is why this will be worth watching through snowbird season. Florida, Arizona and California depend heavily on winter visitors from Canada. If the political chill follows Canadians south through another winter, the consequences will be felt well beyond Washington and Ottawa. CANADIAN WOOL TAKES THE LONG WAY AROUND AMERICA

U.S. tariffs are pushing Canadian wool producers to send raw fibre to China and the Czech Republic for processing, while retailers and mills look for Canadian and European alternatives.

Canadian wool takes the long way around America â€” Canada at War :root{--red:#b51f2a;--deep:#171b24;--paper:#f4f1e9;--gold:#d7ad55}*{box-sizing:border-box}body{margin:0;background:var(--paper);color:#171717;font:18px/1.68 Georgia,serif}a{color:#8e1821}.top{background:var(--red);color:#fff;padding:10px max(18px,calc((100vw - 900px)/2));font:800 11px Arial;letter-spacing:.09em}.word{display:block;padding:18px;text-align:center;letter-spacing:-.035em;font:900 clamp(31px,10vw,60px)/.92 Arial;color:#fff;background:var(--deep);text-decoration:none}.word b{color:var(--red)}.nav{max-width:900px;margin:auto;padding:14px 18px;font:800 12px Arial;text-transform:uppercase}.nav a{text-decoration:none;margin-right:18px;color:var(--deep)}main{max-width:900px;margin:auto;background:#fff;padding:34px 34px 50px;border-top:6px solid var(--red)}.lead-art{display:block;width:100%;max-width:850px;height:auto;margin:0 auto 10px}figcaption{font:12px/1.4 Arial;color:#666;margin:0 auto 28px;max-width:850px}.k{font:800 11px Arial;text-transform:uppercase;letter-spacing:.1em;color:var(--red)}h1{font:900 clamp(42px,7vw,72px)/.94 Arial;margin:.18em 0}h2{font:900 30px/1.05 Arial;margin:1.35em 0 .45em}.dek{font-size:23px;line-height:1.4;color:#333}.meta{font:800 11px Arial;text-transform:uppercase;color:#666;margin-bottom:28px}.analysis{border-left:5px solid var(--gold);padding-left:18px}.sources{margin-top:36px;padding-top:20px;border-top:1px solid #ccc;font:14px/1.6 Arial}.sources b{display:block;text-transform:uppercase;margin-bottom:6px}footer{background:var(--deep);color:#fff;padding:30px max(18px,calc((100vw - 900px)/2));font:12px Arial}footer a{color:#fff}@media(max-width:650px){main{padding:24px 18px 40px}.nav a{display:block;margin:4px 0}} PUBLISHED Â· SEPTEMBER 5, 2026 Â· 11:42 P.M. ET CANADA AT WAR Latest Archive About & methodology Corrections & contact Editorial illustration: Canadian wool is being sent farther afield as U.S. tariffs disrupt a long-established cross-border processing chain. Canadaâ€“U.S. trade Â· Agriculture Â· Manufacturing Â· Diversification CANADIAN WOOL TAKES THE LONG WAY AROUND AMERICA A 50-per-cent U.S. tariff has made an old cross-border wool-processing route much harder to justify. Canadian producers are sending raw fibre to China and the Czech Republic instead â€” while the industry asks why more of the work cannot be done at home. Published Sept. 5, 2026 Â· 11:42 p.m. ET OTTAWA â€” One of the clearest pictures yet of Canadaâ€™s attempt to reduce its dependence on the United States is not an oil tanker, an auto plant or a new trade mission. It is a bale of wool. Canadian wool producers have traditionally depended on American facilities for part of the work that turns freshly shorn fleece into usable fibre. But U.S. tariffs are changing the arithmetic. The Canadian Press reported Saturday that more producers are sending raw wool to China and the Czech Republic as cross-border processing becomes more expensive and uncertain. The issue begins with what the industry calls greasy wool : fleece straight from the sheep, before it has been thoroughly cleaned. Industrial scouring removes dirt and vegetation as well as lanolin, leaving fibre ready for later stages of production. Canada has mills, but far less large-scale processing capacity than the United States. The short route south is no longer necessarily the cheap route The Trump administration imposed additional 50-per-cent duties on selected Canadian goods under Section 338 of the U.S. Tariff Act of 1930. Canadian Press reporting says greasy wool and some finished wool products are among the products caught by the tariff measures. That changes a supply chain built on geography. Sending Canadian fleece south for scouring once made obvious sense: the market was close, the infrastructure already existed and the border linked farms, processors, wholesalers and retailers that had dealt with one another for years. Now the detour can be cheaper than the shortcut. Matthew Rowe, chair of the Canadian Wool Council, told The Canadian Press that producers relying on cross-border processing can no longer assume the old arrangement works economically. The U.S. has also been the largest single market for Canadian wool, making the disruption a problem on both the processing and sales sides. Analysis: This is what trade diversification looks like before it becomes a slogan on a government slide deck. It is not simply finding a new buyer. It can mean rebuilding several links in a chain at once â€” processor, shipper, supplier, retailer and customer â€” and sometimes moving a product thousands of kilometres farther than before. Retailers are looking north and east At Wabi Sabi, an Ottawa yarn shop, owner Judy Enright Smith told The Canadian Press she is looking for more Canadian wool and European products and is prepared to say no to a long-time American supplier. Her decision mixes economics with a broader Canadian consumer reaction to the trade fight. There are domestic examples of what a less U.S.-dependent system can look like. New Brunswickâ€™s Briggs and Little, founded in 1857, buys Canadian wool and carries out its manufacturing in Canada. That shelters it from much of the cross-border processing problem, although the company still relies on some American-made machinery parts and materials. In Manitoba, shepherd and mill owner Anna Hunter has argued for more regional processing capacity so different types of Canadian wool can be cleaned and manufactured closer to where they are produced. Her proposal points to the other side of diversification: foreign alternatives can keep trade moving now, but domestic capacity can reduce the need for detours later. A small industry, a much larger pattern Wool is not one of Canadaâ€™s biggest exports. That is precisely why the story is useful. The same pressure showing up in steel, autos, energy and agriculture is reaching a relatively small rural industry whose supply chain was built around the assumption that the U.S. border would remain predictable. Export Development Canadaâ€™s latest Trade Confidence Index, released this week, found 72 per cent of Canadian exporters plan to pursue new markets over the next two years, up from 65 per cent five months earlier. Nearly a third reported weaker U.S. orders during the previous six months. Nineteen per cent said they were responding by expanding into new export markets. The wool route is a literal version of those numbers. Fibre that might once have travelled a few hundred kilometres south can now travel across an ocean for processing. Retail shelves that once leaned on American brands are being reconsidered. Producers are again discussing whether Canada should rebuild industrial capacity that decades of North American integration made seem unnecessary. None of that makes the transition painless. Longer routes can add shipping time, financing costs and exposure to new risks. Replacing U.S. suppliers does not automatically make a business more efficient. And building Canadian mill capacity takes capital, equipment and skilled workers. But a supply chain can acquire a memory. Once a producer has found a processor in Europe or Asia, once a retailer has found a Canadian substitute, and once a customer has learned to look for a different label, restoring the old commercial relationship is no longer just a matter of removing a tariff. Analysis: That may be the most consequential part of the wool story. The tariffs are not merely collecting money at a border. They are teaching Canadian businesses how to route around it. TRUMP SAYS AMERICA COULD STOP TRADING WITH CANADA. HEREâ€™S WHAT AMERICA WOULD LOSE.

No major study models a literal Canada-U.S. trade shutdown. Research on much smaller disruptions shows the United States would damage its own factories, energy system, farms, exporters and household incomes.

Trump says America could stop trading with Canada. Hereâ€™s what America would lose â€” Canada at War :root{--red:#b51f2a;--deep:#171b24;--paper:#f4f1e9;--gold:#d7ad55}*{box-sizing:border-box}body{margin:0;background:var(--paper);color:#171717;font:18px/1.68 Georgia,serif}a{color:#8e1821}.top{background:var(--red);color:#fff;padding:10px max(18px,calc((100vw - 900px)/2));font:800 11px Arial;letter-spacing:.09em}.word{display:block;padding:18px;text-align:center;letter-spacing:-.035em;font:900 clamp(31px,10vw,60px)/.92 Arial;color:#fff;background:var(--deep);text-decoration:none}.word b{color:var(--red)}.nav{max-width:900px;margin:auto;padding:14px 18px;font:800 12px Arial;text-transform:uppercase}.nav a{text-decoration:none;margin-right:18px;color:var(--deep)}main{max-width:900px;margin:auto;background:#fff;padding:34px 34px 50px;border-top:6px solid var(--red)}.lead-art{display:block;width:100%;max-width:850px;height:auto;margin:0 auto 10px}figcaption{font:12px/1.4 Arial;color:#666;margin:0 auto 28px;max-width:850px}.k{font:800 11px Arial;text-transform:uppercase;letter-spacing:.1em;color:var(--red)}h1{font:900 clamp(42px,7vw,72px)/.94 Arial;margin:.18em 0}h2{font:900 30px/1.05 Arial;margin:1.35em 0 .45em}.dek{font-size:23px;line-height:1.4;color:#333}.meta{font:800 11px Arial;text-transform:uppercase;color:#666;margin-bottom:28px}.analysis{border-left:5px solid var(--gold);padding-left:18px}.sources{margin-top:36px;padding-top:20px;border-top:1px solid #ccc;font:14px/1.6 Arial}.sources b{display:block;text-transform:uppercase;margin-bottom:6px}footer{background:var(--deep);color:#fff;padding:30px max(18px,calc((100vw - 900px)/2));font:12px Arial}footer a{color:#fff}@media(max-width:650px){main{padding:24px 18px 40px}.nav a{display:block;margin:4px 0}} PUBLISHED Â· SEPTEMBER 5, 2026 Â· 8:25 A.M. ET CANADA AT WAR Latest Archive About & methodology Corrections & contact Editorial illustration of a Canadian goose and an American eagle. Analysis Â· Canadaâ€“U.S. trade Â· United States economy Â· Supply chains TRUMP SAYS AMERICA COULD STOP TRADING WITH CANADA. HEREâ€™S WHAT AMERICA WOULD LOSE. No major economic study located for this analysis models the literal zero-trade scenario Donald Trump described Friday. Studies of much smaller disruptions already point in one direction: the United States would damage its own factories, energy system, farms, exporters and household incomes. Published Sept. 5, 2026 Â· 8:25 a.m. ET WASHINGTON â€” Donald Trump did not merely threaten another tariff Friday. In an Oval Office exchange, he said the United States could play â€œhardballâ€ by doing no trade with Canada at all. He claimed the United States would save as much as US$90 billion a year and said Canada, deprived of what he called 95 per cent of its business, would be in â€œa heap of trouble.â€ Canada would unquestionably face a severe shock if its largest trading relationship vanished overnight. The Canadian economy is much smaller, and the United States remains by far its largest export market. But the other half of Trumpâ€™s proposition â€” that the United States could simply stop trading and come out richer â€” runs against the evidence from every serious model of a smaller Canadaâ€“U.S. rupture located for this analysis. Analysis: There is an important limit to what can be claimed. No reputable model located here assumes that every truck, pipeline, rail car, power line, service contract and commercial transaction between Canada and the United States suddenly goes to zero. It would therefore be irresponsible to manufacture a precise GDP or jobs number for Trumpâ€™s literal scenario by multiplying the results of tariff studies. The available research instead tells us what happens under disruptions that are substantially less extreme. The newest model starts with tariffs far below an embargo In August, Oxford Economics published a study commissioned by the Canadian American Business Council examining three possible futures for the bilateral relationship: the mid-2026 tariff status quo, a successful USMCA renegotiation and a breakdown of the agreement with much broader duties. Its harshest case is not an embargo. Trade continues. Even that produces substantial American losses. Oxford estimates that a USMCA breakdown would leave the United States with about 214,000 fewer jobs in 2027 than under the status quo and reduce cumulative U.S. GDP by about US$1 trillion over 2026 to 2035. U.S. exports would be 4.5 per cent lower by 2035. Those figures describe a tariff escalation, not the elimination of trade that Trump floated Friday. The study was commissioned by a business group that supports the cross-border relationship, which matters when assessing its provenance. Oxford says it used a multi-model quantitative approach to examine macroeconomic, industry and regional effects. Its direction is consistent with older academic and policy research: dismantling North American integration hurts all participants, though the percentage damage is larger in Canada and Mexico than in the much larger U.S. economy. Oxfordâ€™s conclusion also cuts directly against the proposition that reducing Canadian trade automatically strengthens American manufacturing. In the breakdown scenario, higher input costs and weaker Canadian demand leave U.S. manufacturing smaller and the U.S. trade balance worse than under the status quo. Two-thirds of the goods trade is not a finished product on a shelf The central fact is what actually crosses the border. Oxford calculates that roughly 65 per cent of bilateral goods trade consists of intermediate inputs: parts, materials and components used to make something else. The Canadaâ€“U.S. border often runs through a production process rather than between a foreign seller and an American consumer. That is why a cutoff would not resemble Americans simply deciding to buy a different brand. A U.S. factory can be a customer of a Canadian supplier in the morning and a supplier to a Canadian plant in the afternoon. Remove one side of that chain and the American plant may lose both an input and a market. The auto industry is the clearest example. North American vehicle production has been integrated for decades. Engines, transmissions, seats, steel, aluminum, electronics and partly assembled vehicles move back and forth among plants centred on Ontario, Michigan, Ohio and surrounding states. Some components cross the border repeatedly before a finished vehicle leaves the line. There is a real-world preview of what happens when just one major crossing stops. During the 2022 Ambassador Bridge blockade, manufacturers on both sides cut production because parts could not reach plants. Public Safety Canada later estimated that one week of disruption at the Windsorâ€“Detroit crossing cost U.S. workers and businesses in the automotive and transportation industries about US$51 million. That was one bridge for one week, not a prohibition on commercial traffic across the continent. The Midwest cannot replace Canadian oil with a mouse click Energy makes the zero-trade scenario still harder. The U.S. Energy Information Administration says Canada remained by far the largest foreign crude-oil supplier to the United States in 2025, averaging about 3.9 million barrels a day. That was roughly three-fifths of all U.S. crude imports. The physical system matters more than the headline number. Alberta heavy crude flows through pipelines to Midwestern refineries, many of which were built or modified to process those grades. An embargo would not mean that millions of identical barrels simply arrive from another country the next morning. Refineries are configured for particular crude types. Pipelines point in particular directions. Replacement barrels require available supply, tanker or pipeline capacity, terminals and refinery compatibility. Some substitution would eventually occur, but the first effect of an abrupt cutoff would be disruption and higher adjustment costs inside the United States. American farmers would lose Canada twice Trumpâ€™s proposition also runs into potash, one of the three fundamental nutrients used in fertilizer. The U.S. Geological Surveyâ€™s 2026 Mineral Commodity Summary puts American net import reliance for potash at 92 per cent of apparent consumption. Canada supplies the overwhelming majority of those imported tonnes. A cutoff would therefore hit American agriculture from two directions. Farmers would lose access to the dominant foreign source of an essential fertilizer input while U.S. agricultural exporters would lose Canadian customers. Alternative suppliers exist, but replacing Canadian volumes would expose U.S. farms to longer supply lines and greater reliance on countries including Russia. That is a recurring feature of the bilateral relationship. Canada is not simply a supplier to the United States. It is also a large customer. An embargo closes both loading docks at once. Lumber, uranium and electricity expose different kinds of dependence Canadian sawmills supply a substantial share of lumber consumed in the United States. The two countries have fought over softwood lumber for decades, but duties are different from removing the supply entirely. A sudden cutoff would tighten an input used in American home construction and renovation while builders and wholesalers tried to replace it. Nuclear power presents a slower-moving but strategically important exposure. U.S. civilian reactors are heavily dependent on imported uranium, and Canada is one of the largest secure suppliers. Reactors would not go dark the day after an embargo because fuel is procured on long timelines, but Washington would be voluntarily eliminating a major friendly source while simultaneously trying to reduce dependence on Russian uranium. Electricity dependence is more regional. The United States as a whole does not depend on Canadian power for a large share of its electricity, but northeastern markets can rely heavily on imports during periods of high demand. EIA reported that Canada supplied an average 14 per cent of New England electricity demand in January 2025. A cutoff would therefore produce concentrated reliability and price questions rather than one uniform national blackout. Then come the American exporters Trump described trade as though an American deficit were money disappearing from the Treasury. It is not. A trade deficit measures the difference between the value of imports and exports; imports are goods and services Americans receive in exchange for payment. Ending trade would also end sales made by American companies to Canadians. Oxford estimates that roughly 1.4 million U.S. jobs are tied to the bilateral commercial relationship. Exposure is uneven. Great Lakes manufacturing is tied to autos and machinery. Gulf Coast producers sell chemicals and industrial products north. Upper Midwest farmers sell grain, meat and processed food. Border states sell electricity, services and manufactured products into Canadian markets. That geographic unevenness matters politically. The national U.S. economy is enormous, so a bilateral shock can look modest as a percentage of total GDP while being severe for particular plants, towns and states. Older research reaches the same direction from different models An academic model published by the National Bureau of Economic Research examined revoking NAFTA. Economists Raphael Auer, BarthÃ©lÃ©my Bonadio and Andrei Levchenko estimated a U.S. welfare loss of about 0.2 per cent, compared with losses around 2 per cent in Canada and Mexico. The aggregate American number was smaller because the U.S. economy is much larger, but sectoral effects inside the United States varied widely. The researchers also found that U.S. congressional districts that gave Trump a higher vote share in 2016 tended to experience larger real-wage reductions in their model. The national average concealed local pain. Brookings modelled another near-neighbour: a 25 per cent U.S. tariff on imports from Canada and Mexico. It estimated about US$45 billion in lost U.S. output without retaliation and roughly US$75 billion if Canada and Mexico retaliated. U.S. employment fell in both cases. Again, trade still continues in that scenario. The US$90-billion arithmetic is not how trade works Trump said the United States would â€œsaveâ€ as much as US$90 billion annually by not trading with Canada. The claim treats a bilateral deficit like a bill the U.S. government pays Canada. It is neither a government expenditure nor the gross cost of the relationship. U.S. government figures put the 2025 U.S. goods deficit with Canada at roughly US$48 billion, partly offset by a large U.S. services surplus. Even if the bilateral deficit were US$90 billion, eliminating imports would not turn that amount into federal savings. Americans would also give up the goods they were buying, while American exporters would lose Canadian sales. Trumpâ€™s assertion that Canada does 95 per cent of its business with the United States is also unsupported by the latest merchandise-trade data. Statistics Canada figures reported for July put the U.S. share of Canadian goods exports at 66.35 per cent, with the year-to-date share at 68 per cent. Canada remains highly exposed to the U.S. market. It is not 95 per cent exposed on that measure. Canada would be hurt more. That does not make America a winner. Every model cited here says Canada bears the larger proportional shock. That is unsurprising: a much larger share of Canadian exports goes to the United States than of U.S. exports goes to Canada. Canada would have to redirect production, find new buyers and endure painful adjustment on a scale the larger U.S. economy would not. But economic interdependence is not a contest in which the larger country keeps the other sideâ€™s losses. The United States would lose Canadian supplies, lose Canadian customers, strand investments, interrupt factories and force costly substitutions. Prices would rise in affected goods. Some production would move; some would simply shrink. Analysis: The defensible conclusion is therefore narrower and stronger than pretending to know the exact cost of a literal embargo. No credible zero-trade estimate located for this report can tell us the precise GDP loss from Trumpâ€™s proposal. What the available evidence does show is that policies dramatically less severe than a full trade cutoff already produce substantial American losses. The first consequences would probably not appear as one dramatic national GDP number. They would appear as missing auto parts in Michigan, crude-supply problems in Midwestern refineries, fertilizer pressure on farms, tighter lumber markets, lost export orders and companies trying to rebuild supply chains that took decades to create. Trump can threaten to stop trade with a sentence. The physical economy on both sides of the border cannot be separated with one. Sources RealClearPolitics â€” transcript/video of Trumpâ€™s Sept. 4 Oval Office remarks Global News â€” video of Trumpâ€™s Canada trade-cutoff remarks Oxford Economics â€” Economic Impacts of US-Canada Tariff Escalation Under the USMCA Canadian American Business Council â€” report summary and commissioning disclosure National Bureau of Economic Research â€” The Economics and Politics of Revoking NAFTA Brookings Institution â€” modelling of 25% Canada/Mexico tariffs and retaliation U.S. Energy Information Administration â€” U.S.â€“Canada energy trade data U.S. Geological Survey â€” Mineral Commodity Summaries 2026 Public Safety Canada â€” economic impact of the 2022 Ambassador Bridge blockade CANADA AT WAR Independent, human-editor-directed publication. MAGAHEADS: TRUMP MOVES FROM TARIFFS TO TALK OF ENDING TRADE WITH CANADA

Fridayâ€™s generic trade threat became explicitly Canadian later in the day, when Trump said Washington could simply stop trading with Canada. Jamieson Greer kept Canada in the public argument in a fresh interview, while Glenn Beck turned the dispute into a mock Canadian-threat preparedness routine.

Published Sept. 5, 2026 Â· 8:25 a.m. ET Â· Updated Sept. 5, 2026 Â· 12:17 p.m. ET WASHINGTON â€” Donald Trump took the weekâ€™s public pressure on Canada into new territory Friday when he moved from threatening countries with trade deficits in general to explicitly describing an end to trade with Canada. Analysis Â· Canadaâ€“U.S. relations Â· Public messaging Â· Trade WASHINGTON CANâ€™T STOP TALKING ABOUT CANADA. OTTAWA HAS STOPPED ANSWERING EVERY VOLLEY. Trump, his economic officials and their media ecosystem have kept Canada in an almost daily public argument. The Canadian government is still meeting, preparing counter-tariffs and leaving the door open to a deal â€” but since Carney spoke Thursday, it has not tried to answer every new American provocation in public. Published Sept. 5, 2026 Â· 8:25 a.m. ET OTTAWA â€” The Canadaâ€“U.S. dispute has developed an increasingly visible imbalance. Washington keeps talking. Ottawa, at least in public, has become much quieter. That does not mean the Canadian government has gone silent or stopped working. Ministers met Friday with the Advisory Committee on Canada-U.S. Economic Relations. Officials continue preparing counter-tariffs scheduled for Tuesday. Prime Minister Mark Carney has said Canada remains open to a stable and credible agreement. But the public tempo is no longer symmetrical. Since formal talks stopped, Donald Trump has posted about Canada, threatened its economy, complained about trade and banks and now said the United States could simply stop trading with the country. Howard Lutnick, Scott Bessent and Jamieson Greer have all carried parts of the administrationâ€™s case into television interviews and other public appearances. Canada has not responded to each intervention. By early Saturday morning, no new public statement from Carney or a senior federal minister had been located answering Trumpâ€™s latest and most explicit threat: that Washington could declare â€œno trading with Canada.â€ Carney answered Thursday The last substantial public answer from the prime minister located for this analysis came Thursday in Thunder Bay. Carney rejected claims from Lutnick and Bessent that his government abandoned a nearly completed trade agreement for domestic political advantage. Carney said he did not regard appointed American cabinet secretaries as authorities on Canadian politics and repeated that Canada would accept a deal only if it was stable, credible and mutually beneficial. He also said some U.S. positions that had been unacceptable appeared to have softened, leaving open the possibility of renewed negotiations. That followed a sharper line earlier in the week, when Carney said the Americans needed to stop doing memes, stop â€œthrowing shadeâ€ and stop trying to look tough if they wanted serious discussions. It was a direct response to the tone surrounding the dispute, but it was not followed by a Canadian answer to every new statement from Washington. Friday brought government activity, not a rhetorical counterattack Dominic LeBlanc chaired Fridayâ€™s meeting of the federal advisory committee on the U.S. economic relationship. Chief negotiator Janice Charette, Finance Minister FranÃ§ois-Philippe Champagne and Industry Minister MÃ©lanie Joly were among the senior officials involved. The government readout said the group discussed the latest U.S. tariffs, Canadaâ€™s countermeasures, assistance for workers and businesses, resilience, diversification and Team Canada engagement. Its tone was institutional and procedural. It did not trade insults with Washington or answer the administrationâ€™s claims one by one. There is an important timing point. The readout preceded Trumpâ€™s later Oval Office remarks explicitly proposing an end to trade with Canada. As of early Saturday, the federal government had not issued a public answer to that escalation that could be located in the official statements and major reporting reviewed. Washingtonâ€™s argument has become a rolling public event Across the border, the dispute has rarely been allowed to sit still. Senior administration figures have repeatedly appeared on television to explain why they say the negotiations failed. The details vary, but a recurring proposition has been visible: Washington offered unusually favourable terms, Canada walked away, and Ottawa will eventually have to return. Greer carried that argument into a Fox appearance this week and then into an extended Financial Times interview published Saturday. Lutnick and Bessent have given their own versions. Trump has supplied the broadest and most volatile interventions, ranging from economic warnings to Fridayâ€™s explicit trade-cutoff language. Friday added a new category. Trump began the day by threatening to stop trade with countries running deficits with the United States unless the Federal Reserve cut interest rates. Later, in the Oval Office, he explicitly used Canada as an example of a country with which the United States could simply stop trading. He said such a move would save the United States as much as US$90 billion and claimed Canada conducts 95 per cent of its business with the United States. The latest merchandise-trade data put the U.S. share of Canadian goods exports at 66.35 per cent in July, not 95 per cent. Silence can be observed. Motive cannot. Analysis: It would be easy to label Ottawaâ€™s restraint a deliberate communications strategy: deny Trump the reaction he seeks, stop responding to every threat and let Tuesdayâ€™s counter-tariffs speak for themselves. There is not enough evidence to state that as fact. The observable fact is simpler. The American administration is conducting a large share of the dispute in public, through presidential remarks, social-media posts and repeated interviews. The Canadian government is not matching that cadence. It continues to act and occasionally speak, but it has not tried to answer every American volley. There are several possible explanations. Ottawa may be trying to preserve room for negotiations. Officials may have concluded that responding to each statement only lengthens the argument. They may be waiting for a concrete policy rather than reacting to threats. Or the quieter period may simply reflect the normal rhythm of a government heading into a weekend after several days of public appearances. Without confirmation from ministers or officials, choosing among those explanations would turn inference into reporting. The next Canadian statement will tell us more The quiet period is therefore useful as an observation, not yet as a theory. It becomes more meaningful the longer it continues and the more the American rhetoric escalates without a Canadian answer. Tuesday provides a fixed deadline. Canadaâ€™s announced retaliatory tariffs on C$27.6 billion in U.S. goods are scheduled to take effect at 12:01 a.m. Sept. 8. Unless the policy changes, Ottawa is about to move from words to another material economic measure. That creates two very different public tracks. In Washington, senior officials and the president continue narrating the dispute in real time. In Ottawa, the governmentâ€™s most consequential near-term response is already written into a tariff schedule. Whether that contrast is intentional will require evidence. For now, the contrast itself is the story. Published Sept. 4, 2026 Â· 11:50 a.m. ET Â· Updated Sept. 5, 2026 Â· 8:25 a.m. ET WASHINGTON â€” President Donald Trumpâ€™s Friday threat to stop trade with countries that run trade deficits with the United States became explicitly Canada-specific later in the day. In an Oval Office media availability, Trump said the United States could play â€œhardballâ€ by declaring that it would do no trade with Canada. â€œIf we were playing hardball, all weâ€™d do is say weâ€™re going to do no trading with Canada,â€ Trump said. He claimed that ending the relationship would save the United States between US$60 billion and US$90 billion a year and said Canada would be in â€œa heap of trouble.â€ He added: â€œI donâ€™t think theyâ€™d exist.â€ Trump also said Canada conducts 95 per cent of its business with the United States. The latest merchandise-trade data do not support that figure if he meant exports: Statistics Canada data reported for July put the U.S. share of Canadian goods exports at 66.35 per cent and the year-to-date share at 68 per cent. The later remarks materially changed the statement Trump made Friday morning. His initial threat connected access to the American market to a monetary-policy decision made inside the United States while leaving unanswered which countries, products or legal mechanism he had in mind. At that point, he had not named Canada. U.S. trade figures show a substantial American goods deficit with Canada that is partly offset by a U.S. surplus in services. That makes the presidentâ€™s description of a US$60-billion to US$90-billion â€œsavingâ€ misleading. A trade deficit is the difference between imports and exports, not a federal expenditure. Ending imports would also mean Americans no longer receive the goods and services they were buying, while U.S. exporters would lose Canadian customers. The bilateral relationship includes crude oil, autos and auto parts, machinery, agricultural products, electricity, minerals and large volumes of intermediate goods used in production on both sides of the border. The Federal Reserve sets U.S. monetary policy and foreign governments have no role in its interest-rate decisions. Trump has repeatedly pressed the central bank for lower rates, but Fridayâ€™s statements put a foreign-trade consequence directly behind that demand. Trump also invoked the Supreme Courtâ€™s February ruling on his emergency tariffs and characterized it as recognizing an â€œabsolute rightâ€ for the president to stop trade. The decision itself was narrower: in Learning Resources v. Trump , the court held that the International Emergency Economic Powers Act does not authorize the president to impose tariffs. IEEPA separately contains powers to regulate and, in qualifying emergencies, block or prohibit certain import and export transactions. The court did not hold that a president has an unrestricted power to terminate trade with any country simply because the United States runs a deficit with it. For Canada, the threat arrives inside an already active tariff dispute. Washington imposed 50-per-cent tariffs on C$27.6 billion of Canadian goods on Aug. 22, and Ottawa has announced counter-tariffs on C$27.6 billion of U.S. imports beginning Sept. 8. The two governments have also given different accounts of why formal trade talks stopped Aug. 21. Senior U.S. officials say Canada sought additional concessions after being offered unusually favourable terms; Prime Minister Mark Carney says the U.S. position remained unacceptable on several issues and that any agreement must provide stability and credibility. Trump had already added a Canada-specific warning Thursday, saying Canadian politicians could make him â€œthe enemyâ€ until Canadaâ€™s economy collapsed. Fridayâ€™s later remarks went further by describing the elimination of bilateral trade itself as leverage that he said the United States could use. What remains unknown is whether the threat will become policy, what authority the administration believes would permit a complete Canada trade cutoff, what transactions would be covered and whether the statement is intended as an actual policy option or as pressure on the Federal Reserve. The White House had not publicly supplied those details by early Saturday. United States Â· Justice Department Â· Canadaâ€“U.S. relations Â· Analysis DOJ CALLS â€˜PAUSE ON CANADAâ€™ A MISUNDERSTANDING â€” AS WASHINGTON LURCHES THROUGH A DAY OF ERRATIC POLICY A written antitrust instruction told staff to stop cooperation with Canada and inventory every area of joint work. Published Sept. 4, 2026 Â· 4:52 p.m. ET WASHINGTON â€” The U.S. Justice Department says a written instruction telling Antitrust Division staff to stop cooperative work with Canadian authorities was a misunderstanding, not a change in U.S. policy toward Canada. The Wall Street Journal, which reviewed the internal emails, reported Friday that Justice Department spokeswoman Emily Covington said the message had misunderstood an instruction to postpone a scheduled meeting and that there is no formal pause in antitrust cooperation. That clarification came after an unusually broad internal directive had already circulated. According to the Journal, Lynda Marshall, who heads the Antitrust Divisionâ€™s international section, sent an email Wednesday with the subject line â€œPause on Canadaâ€ directing officials to stop cooperative case work and policy engagement with Canadian authorities. On Friday, section chiefs were also told to identify every area in which the division cooperates with Canada. The distinction is important. The United States has not, on the evidence now available, severed antitrust cooperation with Canada, and there is no evidence that the entire Justice Department or the Federal Trade Commission stopped working with Canadian counterparts. What did happen is narrower but still significant: a senior official sent a written instruction that staff understood as a broad Canada pause, and the department later said the instruction did not mean what it appeared to say. Two days of confusion over a decades-old relationship Competition-law cooperation between the two countries is not a recent political arrangement. Canada and the United States signed a formal antitrust cooperation agreement in August 1995, building on an earlier 1984 understanding. The agreement says it is in both countriesâ€™ common interest to cooperate in detecting anticompetitive conduct, sharing information where law permits, locating evidence and witnesses, coordinating enforcement and avoiding unnecessary conflicts. That architecture has survived governments of different parties in both countries. Canadaâ€™s Competition Bureau, the U.S. Justice Department and the Federal Trade Commission have also published joint merger-cooperation practices describing years of coordination when transactions affect both markets, precisely because cross-border cases can become slower, more expensive and less coherent when agencies work in isolation. What the reversal says â€” and does not say Analysis: A mistaken email is not proof of a deliberate policy to dismantle Canadaâ€“U.S. law-enforcement ties, and the evidence does not establish that Donald Trump, the White House or senior Justice Department leadership ordered a Canada cutoff. But a misunderstanding that can generate a written halt instruction at this level is itself a governance problem: countries, companies and investigators depend not only on the substance of U.S. policy but on confidence that consequential instructions mean what they say. For Canada, that makes predictability part of the bilateral risk. A relationship built over decades can absorb disagreements, tariffs and sharp rhetoric; it becomes harder to manage when officials must also determine whether a sweeping instruction is policy, an error, a meeting postponement or something that may be reversed after it reaches the press. Hours earlier, Trump threatened trade to pressure the Fed Fridayâ€™s antitrust confusion arrived on the same day President Donald Trump made a separate threat aimed at the Federal Reserve. After a stronger-than-expected U.S. jobs report increased market expectations that the Fed could raise rates later this month, Trump wrote on Truth Social that the United States should have the lowest interest rate in the world and threatened to stop trading with countries with which the United States runs a trade deficit unless the Fed lowers rates. The threat would use foreign commerce as leverage in a dispute between the president and an American institution. The Federal Reserve says its monetary-policy decisions do not require approval from the president or anyone else in the executive or legislative branches, and that Congress structured the system so those decisions can be based on long-term economic goals rather than short-term political pressure. The July Federal Open Market Committee meeting illustrates the conflict Trump was trying to influence. The committee kept rates unchanged, while Beth Hammack, Neel Kashkari and Lorie Logan dissented because they preferred a quarter-point increase. Fridayâ€™s strong employment report then pushed traders toward greater expectations of a September hike, directly opposite the cut Trump demanded. A day of policy by threat, then reversal Analysis: The Justice Department episode and Trumpâ€™s Fed threat are separate events, and nothing in the reporting shows a common cause. They nevertheless produced, within one news cycle, two striking displays of policy disorder: one arm of government circulated a consequential Canada instruction and then said it had been misunderstood, while the president threatened disruption of international trade as leverage against a domestic institution designed to resist presidential direction. Calling that a coherent anti-Canada strategy would go beyond the evidence. Calling it a serious deterioration in administrative discipline and policy predictability does not. The relevant facts are the breadth of the antitrust instruction, its subsequent disavowal, the lack of an initial explanation, and a presidential trade threat whose stated purpose was to force an independent central bank toward the rate decision he prefers. The danger for Canada is not only a particular tariff or a particular insult. It is the possibility that major U.S. decisions affecting Canadian businesses and institutions can now arrive through abrupt threats, internally confused instructions and rapid reversals, leaving Ottawa to plan around a government whose position may be difficult to distinguish from its latest message. What remains unknown The Justice Department has not publicly explained how an instruction to postpone a meeting became an email directing staff to pause Canada cooperation, who supplied the original instruction, or whether any active case was delayed while the message stood. Nothing reviewed for this report shows that the FTC received a comparable instruction, and no Canadian government response to the episode had appeared in the public sources reviewed by late Friday afternoon. Trump has likewise not explained how a trade cutoff tied to Federal Reserve policy would be implemented, which measure of bilateral deficits he would use, or whether the threat will become policy. The strongest conclusion available Friday is therefore not that Washington actually broke antitrust ties with Canada â€” according to the Justice Department, it did not â€” but that the United States produced two consequential episodes in one day that made its decision-making look substantially less predictable. ET CANADA AT WAR Latest About & methodology Corrections & contact Fact check Â· Banking Â· Canadaâ€“U.S. trade TRUMP SAYS U.S. BANKS ARE BARRED FROM CANADA. FIFTEEN ARE ALREADY HERE. Trump said Canada does not allow American banks to operate there. AP found 15 U.S.-based banks already doing business in Canada, and Canadaâ€™s banking regulator publishes a formal route for foreign branches. Published Sept. 4, 2026 Â· 11:50 a.m. ET WASHINGTON â€” Donald Trump said this week that Canada does not allow American banks to operate there. The claim is false: U.S.-based banks already operate in Canada as branches and subsidiaries, and Canadaâ€™s federal banking regulator publishes a formal process for foreign banks seeking to establish branches. Trump made the claim Wednesday while answering a question about renewed Canada-U.S. trade negotiations during an Oval Office meeting with travel-industry executives. â€œWe donâ€™t have our banks there. You know why? They donâ€™t allow it,â€ he said. An Associated Press fact check found 15 U.S.-based banks operating in Canada as branches or subsidiaries. They include JPMorgan Chase, Citibank, Bank of America, Capital One and Wells Fargo, among other institutions serving Canadian corporate, institutional or consumer markets. Trump also referred to the Bank of Canada while talking about Canadian banks. The Bank of Canada is the countryâ€™s central bank, responsible for monetary policy and financial-system functions; it is not a commercial bank competing for deposits and loans in the United States. Canada does regulate foreign banks differently depending on how they enter the market. Foreign-owned subsidiaries can operate as Canadian-incorporated Schedule II banks, while authorized foreign bank branches operate under Schedule III of the Bank Act. The Office of the Superintendent of Financial Institutions maintains a guide specifically for establishing a foreign bank branch in Canada. Full-service foreign branches face restrictions on some deposit-taking activities and lending branches have narrower powers, but those rules are a regulatory framework, not a prohibition on American banks. The Canadian Bankers Association told AP that U.S.-based bank branches and subsidiaries hold about C$124.6 billion in assets in Canada. Federal Reserve data cited by AP also show eight Canadian banks operating in the United States, so the cross-border banking relationship runs in both directions. The number of large American retail-bank storefronts in Canada is smaller than the U.S. banking industryâ€™s size might suggest. Banking specialists cited by AP pointed to the size of the Canadian market, established domestic competitors and regulatory costs as factors affecting where foreign institutions choose to build retail networks. The factual distinction matters if banking becomes part of the trade dispute. Canada can be criticized for particular banking rules or market-access conditions, but that is different from saying American banks are not allowed into the country when multiple U.S. institutions already operate under the existing regime. Trump did not identify a U.S. bank that Canada had refused to admit, and the White House did not provide AP with evidence supporting his claim. The public regulatory record instead shows an established foreign-bank licensing system and existing American participants. ET CANADA AT WAR Latest About & methodology Corrections & contact Canada Â· Employment Â· Tariffs Â· Economy CANADA LOSES 42,000 JOBS â€” BEFORE THE NEWEST U.S. TARIFFS ENTERED THE DATA Full-time employment accounted for most of the decline and unemployment held at 6.4%. The survey reference week ran Aug. 9â€“15, before Washingtonâ€™s latest 50% tariffs took effect Aug. 22. Published Sept. 4, 2026 Â· 11:50 a.m. ET Canada lost 42,000 jobs in August, with most of the decline coming from full-time work, while the national unemployment rate held at 6.4 per cent. The result was substantially weaker than economists had expected after several months of employment growth. Reutersâ€™ survey of economists had pointed to an increase of about 15,000 jobs. Its seasonally adjusted calculation put the actual decline at 41,700, including 35,900 full-time positions and 5,800 part-time positions; Statistics Canada reports the overall change as 42,000 after rounding. The employment rate â€” the share of the population aged 15 and older that is working â€” fell 0.1 percentage points to 60.8 per cent. Youth unemployment was little changed at 12.9 per cent, while average hourly wages were 2.0 per cent higher than a year earlier, down from 2.8 per cent annual growth in July. The timing of the survey separates these numbers from the newest round of U.S. tariffs. The August Labour Force Survey reference week ran from Aug. 9 through Aug. 15; Washingtonâ€™s 50-per-cent tariffs on C$27.6 billion of Canadian goods did not take effect until Aug. 22. Fridayâ€™s report therefore does not measure job losses caused by the Aug. 22 tariffs. It provides a labour-market reading immediately before that escalation, giving future surveys a cleaner point of comparison as the new duties and Canadaâ€™s Sept. 8 counter-tariffs work through affected industries. Ontario and Quebec accounted for most of the provincial decline. Employment fell by 18,000 in Ontario and 19,000 in Quebec, while New Brunswick gained about 2,400 jobs and employment was little changed in the other provinces. The industry numbers moved in different directions. Manufacturing employment increased by 22,000, while business, building and other support services lost 20,000 jobs, public administration lost 8,800, natural resources lost 7,700 and utilities lost 5,600. Public-sector employment fell by another 20,000 in August, its third consecutive monthly decline, and Statistics Canada says it has fallen by 78,000 since May. Despite the August drop, total employment remained 217,000 higher than a year earlier. A separate August indicator pointed toward stronger activity. The seasonally adjusted Ivey Purchasing Managers Index rose to 64.3 from 55.1 in July, its highest level since May 2022, while its employment gauge increased to 55.0 from 52.2. The two measures answer different questions. The Labour Force Survey estimates how many people are employed across the population, while the Ivey index records month-to-month changes reported by purchasing managers; stronger purchasing activity can therefore coexist with a decline in total employment. Other recent indicators have also been mixed, leaving no single August measure that describes the entire economy. What Fridayâ€™s labour report establishes is narrower: employment weakened before the latest U.S. tariff round was present in the survey period, with the largest provincial losses in Ontario and Quebec and manufacturing employment moving in the opposite direction. ET CANADA AT WAR Latest About & methodology Corrections & contact Tariffs Â· Small business Â· Survey CFIB REPORTS FINANCIAL PRESSURE AFTER TARIFF ESCALATION Among businesses affected by the trade war, CFIB says 18% of small exporters and 11% of importers surveyed would no longer be financially viable if it lasted at least three months. The figures are reported expectations, not observed closures. Published Sept. 3, 2026 Â· 1:00 p.m. ET New preliminary results from the Canadian Federation of Independent Business point to mounting concern among small firms after the latest Canadaâ€“U.S. tariff escalation. Among surveyed businesses affected by the trade war, CFIB said 18% of small exporters and 11% of importers expected they would cease being financially viable if the situation lasted three months or more. The survey had 1,545 respondents and was conducted from Aug. 28 onward, after the latest escalation. That timing makes it a newer read on sentiment than the Export Development Canada survey released earlier Wednesday, but the CFIB results measure businessesâ€™ stated assessments of future viability. They are not a count of closures, a representative forecast, or evidence that those firms have already failed. CFIB said 46% of small exporters and 49% of small importers responding to the survey had products directly affected by the latest tariffs or counter-tariffs. It identified manufacturing, wholesale, retail and construction as among the most affected sectors. The organization is proposing a dedicated Small Business Tariff Relief program. Under its proposal, eligible exporters and importers that could document direct tariff payments, or price changes made to absorb the tariff, could receive initial relief of up to $70,000. That is CFIBâ€™s policy proposal, not an approved government benefit. The federation also called for a tariff-remissions desk for small and medium-sized firms and broad tax changes, including a reduction in the small-business corporate tax rate. CFIBâ€™s release and methodology should be read with the figures: the survey captures the views of firms that responded during an active trade dispute, not the experience of every Canadian small business. Its significance is that the responses were collected after the escalation and quantify the financial strain respondents say they expect if it persists. ET CANADA AT WAR Latest About & methodology Corrections & contact Buy Canadian Â· VIA Rail Â· Manufacturing OTTAWA COMMITS $4.7 BILLION TO BUILD 313 VIA RAIL CARS IN CANADA The passenger cars will be produced and assembled in Thunder Bay and Quebec, returning VIA passenger-car production to Canada after four decades as Ottawa expands its Buy Canadian procurement policy. Published Sept. 3, 2026 Â· 5:32 p.m. ET OTTAWA â€” The federal government will spend more than $4.7 billion for VIA Rail to acquire and maintain 313 new passenger rail cars from Alstom Canada, Prime Minister Mark Carney announced Thursday in Thunder Bay. Ottawa says it is the largest investment in VIA Rail's history and will bring passenger-car production back to Canada for the first time in four decades. The cars will be manufactured and assembled at Alstom facilities in Thunder Bay, Ontario, and La PocatiÃ¨re, Quebec, with design and engineering work in Saint-Bruno-de-Montarville, Quebec. The Prime Minister's Office estimates the project will support nearly 700 jobs in Ontario and Quebec and generate more than $1.6 billion in economic benefits. The 313-car order is intended for VIA's long-distance, regional and remote routes and will include coaches, dining cars, panorama cars and sleepers. Some of the equipment it is expected to replace is more than 70 years old. VIA says much of its stainless-steel long-distance fleet was built by the Budd Company in Philadelphia in 1954 and 1955 for Canadian Pacific and was acquired by VIA when it took over CP passenger operations in 1978. Carney used the announcement to draw a direct comparison with that history. Reuters quoted him saying that cars previously built in the United States would now be built in Thunder Bay. The Prime Minister's Office also framed the procurement as part of the federal Buy Canadian policy and its effort to reduce dependence on any single trading partner. Canadian production of VIA passenger cars did not end with the Budd fleet. VIA's LRC coaches were built by Bombardier between 1981 and 1984. Alstom acquired Bombardier Transportation in 2021. The new order therefore marks the return of VIA passenger-car production to Canada after roughly four decades, but it should not be described as Alstom having built VIA's early-1980s fleet under its current corporate ownership. The government says Alstom is expected to draw on a network of more than 900 Canadian suppliers and to maximize Canadian steel in structural assemblies, supports and fabricated metal components. Those figures and sourcing commitments are government and company projections for the program rather than completed procurement results. The passenger-car order follows a separate $1.95-billion package announced in July for VIA's locomotive renewal. That package includes $1.6 billion for 45 locomotives from Swiss manufacturer Stadler, with final assembly of up to 36 locomotives in Canada, and $357 million for an assembly and maintenance facility in Montreal. Ottawa says the combined fleet-renewal investment now exceeds $6.6 billion. The VIA program predates the latest breakdown in Canada-U.S. trade talks. The locomotive program was announced before the Aug. 21 collapse in negotiations, and government materials reviewed for this story do not indicate that VIA cancelled an existing U.S. contract for the 313 passenger cars. Thursday's announcement instead places an existing fleet-renewal need inside the government's broader Buy Canadian industrial strategy. That announcement came less than two weeks after the United States imposed a new round of tariffs on Canadian goods and Canada announced matching counter-tariffs. Ottawa's current counter-tariff package covers C$27.6 billion of U.S. goods and is scheduled to take effect Sept. 8. The government has presented federal procurement as one tool for shifting more production, supply contracts and industrial capacity into Canada. The VIA order establishes the spending commitment and the intended Canadian production sites; the jobs, supplier purchases and economic benefits will depend on the program's execution over the life of the contract. Tariffs Â· Manufacturing Â· Industrial policy Â· Winnipeg OTTAWA PUTS $15.9 MILLION INTO WINNIPEG MANUFACTURING AFTER U.S. TARIFF ESCALATION Fifteen projects at 11 Winnipeg organizations will finance machinery, automation, in-house production and expansion into new markets. Some of the federal support is non-repayable; some must be repaid. Published Sept. 3, 2026 Â· 2:32 p.m. ET Ottawa is putting $15.9 million into 15 manufacturing projects led by 11 Winnipeg organizations as part of its response to the latest escalation in the Canadaâ€“U.S. tariff dispute. The money, announced Thursday by Prairies Economic Development Canada, is split between the Regional Tariff Response Initiative and the federal Business Scale-up and Productivity program. The project descriptions cover new machinery, factory automation, digital systems, additional production space, workforce training and efforts to sell into new markets. The financing is not all the same: several Regional Tariff Response Initiative contributions are non-repayable, while other contributions under that program and the productivity program are repayable. Fort Garry Fire Trucks is receiving two separate contributions. A $999,575 non-repayable tariff-response contribution is intended to bring more component production in-house, including pump components and plastic tanks, while improving digital systems and testing. A second contribution of $1,114,651, which is repayable, is for fabrication, welding, rubber-manufacturing and painting equipment. Company president James Peters said the financing would help â€œbring the manufacture of key fire truck components â€¦ back into Canada.â€ The federal backgrounder says those components are currently sourced internationally; it does not say that all of that sourcing is from the United States. Smartrend Manufacturing Group is also receiving two contributions: $1 million in non-repayable tariff-response funding to expand into new markets, add sales, marketing and regulatory staff and train workers, and $2,087,916 in repayable productivity financing to automate part of its electronics production line and launch two products using its illuminated-sign technology. City Sheet Metal is receiving $570,025 in non-repayable funding to upgrade aluminum and steel production equipment, increase production, train workers and expand sales in Western Canada. Other projects show the range of industrial activity covered by the announcement. Argus Industries is receiving $507,179 in non-repayable funding to build and equip a 5,000-square-foot production space for specialized aerospace and defence seals. International Truck Body is receiving $1 million in non-repayable support and $1,119,407 in repayable financing for a new step-van manufacturing line. Micro Tool & Machine is receiving $536,105 in non-repayable support to modernize digital planning and production systems. The federal government says the 15 projects support more than 358 jobs. That figure is the governmentâ€™s description of jobs associated with the projects; it is not a count of 358 newly created jobs or evidence that every announced expansion has already been completed. The announcement comes from a province whose manufacturing economy has been tightly linked to the United States for more than three decades. PrairiesCan noted that when the North American Free Trade Agreement took effect in 1994, Manitobaâ€™s exports to the United States grew faster than those of any other province, with machinery and transportation equipment among the areas posting large gains. By December 2025, Manitoba manufacturing employed 67,700 people, about nine per cent of the provincial workforce, according to the federal release. The federal government placed Thursdayâ€™s financing directly inside its account of the current trade conflict. Ottawa says the United States imposed additional tariffs on Canadian goods two weeks ago and that Canada suspended negotiations rather than accept terms the government considered damaging to Canadian workers, strategic industries and sovereignty. Canada has announced matching counter-tariffs. Those descriptions of the negotiating dispute are the federal governmentâ€™s position; no negotiating text accompanying Thursdayâ€™s Winnipeg announcement was released. The Winnipeg financing is part of a wider support package announced Aug. 25. Ottawa added $1.5 billion to the Regional Tariff Response Initiative and said the measure formed part of $7.5 billion in new and expanded support for tariff-affected workers and businesses. The package also included a $2-billion Canada Strong Diversification Fund, $500 million in new Business Development Bank of Canada liquidity, $3.5 billion in Rapid Response Supports for Workers and Employers and changes to the Large Enterprise Tariff Loan facility. The project-by-project breakdown shows how some of that tariff-response policy is being translated into factory spending. In Winnipeg, the funded changes include replacing internationally sourced components with Canadian production, adding automated equipment, expanding manufacturing lines and paying companies to pursue customers in different markets. The announcement establishes the financing and the intended projects; the resulting production shifts, sales and employment will depend on their implementation. A SASKATCHEWAN COPPER-ZINC MINE IS NOW PART OF CANADAâ€™S NATIONAL-SECURITY STRATEGY

McIlvenna Bay is ramping toward commercial production after major federal backing and an Eldorado acquisition. Ottawa explicitly links its copper and zinc to national security, advanced manufacturing and supply-chain resilience.

A SASKATCHEWAN COPPER-ZINC MINE IS NOW PART OF CANADAâ€™S NATIONAL-SECURITY STRATEGY Current page September 3, 2026 McIlvenna Bay is ramping toward commercial production after major federal backing and an Eldorado acquisition. Ottawa explicitly links its copper and zinc to national security, advanced manufacturing and supply-chain resilience. Copper ore. 787IYO / Wikimedia Commons, CC0. Critical minerals Â· Saskatchewan Â· Industrial policy Â· National security A SASKATCHEWAN COPPER-ZINC MINE IS NOW PART OF CANADAâ€™S NATIONAL-SECURITY STRATEGY McIlvenna Bay is ramping toward commercial production after major federal backing and an Eldorado acquisition. Ottawa explicitly links its copper and zinc to national security, advanced manufacturing and supply-chain resilience. Published Sept. 2, 2026 Â· 9:23 p.m. ET Ottawa is describing a new Saskatchewan copper-zinc mine in terms that would once have sounded more at home in a defence briefing than a mining announcement. Natural Resources Canada said Wednesday that production is under way at the McIlvenna Bay mine in the Flin Flon Greenstone Belt and explicitly tied the project to national security, advanced manufacturing, clean-energy technologies and stronger supply chains. The mine is not suddenly beginning from zero on Sept. 2. Eldorado Gold, which acquired Foran Mining earlier this year, announced first copper concentrate in June and zinc and pyrite concentrate followed in July. The federal announcement marks the projectâ€™s continuing ramp-up toward expected commercial production in the third quarter, with more than 400,000 tonnes of material already available for processing. That distinction matters because the larger story is not a ribbon-cutting date. It is the way the federal government now talks about domestic mineral production. Copper and zinc are being treated as strategic inputs whose availability affects power grids, vehicles, defence systems, communications equipment and advanced manufacturing. In that framework, a mine in Saskatchewan is part of Canadaâ€™s ability to withstand disruptions abroad and supply allies without relying entirely on foreign sources. McIlvenna Bay is expected to operate for more than 18 years. Eldorado has said 2026 production could include five million to 10 million pounds of copper, 3,000 to 6,000 tonnes of zinc, 5,000 to 10,000 ounces of gold and 100,000 to 200,000 ounces of silver as the operation ramps up. The company is also studying an expansion from roughly 4,900 tonnes of ore per day to about 7,000 tonnes, along with a possible silver-lead circuit. The project has become a showcase for a more interventionist Canadian industrial policy. The federal government committed $20 million through the Critical Minerals Infrastructure Fund in December 2024, another $41 million through the Strategic Innovation Fund in January 2025 and, through the Canada Growth Fund, approximately $156 million in May 2025. The project was also referred to the Major Projects Office in September 2025. Those numbers help explain why Ottawa is presenting the mine as more than a private commercial venture. Governments are increasingly deciding that access to certain minerals is too important to leave entirely to the short-term economics of commodity cycles. Canada has large reserves, but deposits do not create strategic independence unless mines, processing capacity, transport links and customers actually exist. The labour dimension is substantial as well. Natural Resources Canada says McIlvenna Bay currently employs about 380 full-time workers and is expected to employ about 450 when commercial production is reached. Roughly 34 per cent of the workforce is Indigenous. The federal release describes the project as being developed with Indigenous partners, an important part of how Ottawa is trying to connect critical-minerals policy with regional employment and participation. Eldoradoâ€™s acquisition of Foran in April also changes the ownership context. The Vancouver-headquartered gold producer bought the company that had developed McIlvenna Bay, adding the Saskatchewan copper-zinc asset to a larger international mining portfolio. That gives the project greater corporate scale, but it also reinforces the need to distinguish where a mine is physically located from who controls capital, processing decisions and downstream relationships. For Canada, the sovereignty question is therefore broader than whether ore comes out of Canadian ground. The strategic value depends on whether Canada can process enough of it, move it through resilient infrastructure, retain skilled workers, build domestic manufacturing around it and diversify the customers who buy it. A mine can reduce dependence only if the rest of the chain does not recreate the same vulnerability somewhere else. McIlvenna Bay is an early example of the direction Ottawa is taking. The language of critical minerals has moved decisively from economic development into economic security. When the federal government calls copper and zinc part of national security, it is acknowledging that the ability to produce ordinary industrial materials at home can become a form of strategic power. CANADA AND EUROPE ARE BUNDLING DEFENCE, DIGITAL TRADE AND CRITICAL MINERALS Current page September 3, 2026 Anita Anand and Kaja Kallas have put defence cooperation, SAFE, digital trade, critical minerals and economic security into the same Canadaâ€“EU strategic package ahead of an October summit in Canada. Anita Anand and Kaja Kallas at the 2025 G7 foreign ministers meeting in Canada. European Commission Audiovisual Service / Wikimedia Commons, CC BY 4.0. Canadaâ€“EU Â· Defence Â· Digital trade Â· Critical minerals CANADA AND EUROPE ARE BUNDLING DEFENCE, DIGITAL TRADE AND CRITICAL MINERALS The latest Anandâ€“Kallas readout shows something larger than another diplomatic meeting: Ottawa and Brussels are assembling security, industry, technology and trade into a single strategic relationship. Published Sept. 2, 2026 Â· 9:23 p.m. ET Canada and the European Union are moving several relationships that once sat in separate policy boxes into the same strategic package. In a readout released Wednesday, Foreign Affairs Minister Anita Anand and EU foreign-policy chief Kaja Kallas pointed to progress on the Canadaâ€“EU Security and Defence Partnership, Canadian participation in Europeâ€™s SAFE defence-financing framework, negotiations toward a digital trade agreement, critical-minerals cooperation and broader economic security. The list is important because of what it combines. Defence procurement is no longer being discussed in isolation from industrial capacity. Digital trade is no longer simply an extension of conventional commerce. Critical minerals are being treated as security assets as well as export commodities. Canada and the EU are increasingly talking about all of them as parts of the same problem: how democratic partners can become more resilient when supply chains, technology platforms and strategic industries are exposed to political pressure. Anand met Kallas during the informal meeting of EU foreign ministers, known as the Gymnich, in Wicklow, Ireland. Global Affairs Canada had already described Anandâ€™s participation as historic: it was the first time a Canadian foreign minister had taken part in a Gymnich meeting. The Sept. 2 readout now gives a clearer picture of what Canada wants to build from that access. The most immediate defence component is SAFE, the European Unionâ€™s Security Action for Europe financial instrument. Canada and the EU are working on closer defence-industrial cooperation through the program. For Canada, that matters because access to European defence procurement and financing could give Canadian companies another large market and another set of industrial relationships at a moment when Ottawa is trying to reduce strategic dependence on the United States. The digital component may prove just as important. Canada and the EU are negotiating a digital trade agreement intended to complement CETA. A previous Canadaâ€“EU Digital Partnership Council statement said the two sides want to modernize digital trade rules while also cooperating on digital credentials, standards, online-platform governance and foreign information manipulation. That makes the eventual agreement potentially relevant not only to trade volumes but to Canadaâ€™s ability to shape rules for data, platforms and emerging technologies with partners other than Washington. Critical minerals sit between the defence and digital files. Copper, nickel, lithium, rare earths and other strategic materials are necessary for military systems, grids, batteries, semiconductors and advanced manufacturing. Canada has reserves and mining expertise; Europe has large industrial demand and an explicit interest in reducing vulnerable dependencies. The Anandâ€“Kallas readout says cooperation on critical minerals and economic security has already intensified. The political language is also notable. The ministers described Canada and the EU as â€œtrusted partnersâ€ and emphasized resilience on both sides of the Atlantic. That is standard diplomatic language, but the policy content underneath it is concrete. Canada is looking for durable alternatives and supplements to a continental relationship in which access to the U.S. market, technology and defence system can also become leverage. Europe is looking for reliable suppliers, defence partners and technology relationships of its own. The relationship will get another test soon. Anand and Kallas said preparations are under way for a Canadaâ€“EU summit in Canada on Oct. 29 and 30. That gives Ottawa and Brussels less than two months to convert broad strategic alignment into specific agreements, procurement access, financing rules and timelines. For Canada, the central question is whether Europe becomes a genuine second strategic pillar or remains primarily a diplomatic counterweight. The newest readout suggests Ottawa is trying to make the relationship operational. Defence finance, digital trade, critical minerals and economic security are now being discussed together because the same sovereignty question runs through all of them: how many essential Canadian capabilities can be diversified away from dependence on a single foreign system.
